ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-17.ana.ud213e178	The increase is to be considered today by the Committee on Agriculture and Rural Development for the 2014-2015 period, and I am delighted to present the Committee’s position. The agricultural situation in 2014 relates to capped appropriations. 1150 for the calendar year 2015 and re-disposals within the adopted budget for 2014. Furthermore, the settlement includes target prices for the period 1 July 2014 to 30 June 2015 and some other provisions. The main political basis for the settlement is the government's political platform, the cooperation agreement and the handling of the coup d'état. 1150 and 4150 for the implementation of the agricultural agreement, etc. in the additionalproposal for the State budget 2014. In the review of the State budget for 2014, the Industry Committee also refers to the Notification. St. 9 for 2011–2012 and Innst. 234 S for 2011–2012, which was dealt with in Stortinget on 12 April 2012. The present SSC continues a number of joint comments from the Innst. 234 S for 2011-2012 to Innst. 8 S for 2013–2014 as broadly anchored leaders of agricultural policy in the coming years. The requirements of agriculture were submitted on 25 April 2014. The State’s offer was submitted on 6 May 2014 and on 13 May 2014 the plenary meeting of the negotiating committees found that there was a breach of the negotiations and that the government would go to Stortinget with a proposal for an agricultural settlement based on the State’s offer. The agricultural demand had a frame of 1.5 billion. kr. The requirement was financed by 365 million. kr in increased target prices, an increase in capped appropriations. 1150 at 965 million. DKK in the amount available for the contract and SEK 110 million. kr in the increased value of the agricultural deduction. The State negotiating committee presented an offer with the same average income development as other groups, 3,5 pst., according to the SSB. With the forecasts for productivity growth and price developments in areas outside the agreement, the offer had a framework of 150 million. kr. Target price increases corresponding to 340 million were proposed. kr, reduction in capped appropriations. 1150 at 250 million. NOK 60 million. kr available on the contract. The offer would give unchanged value to the agricultural deduction. The main objective of agricultural policy shall be cost-effective and safe food production. The instrument shall be designed to contribute to increased production and work shall be carried out to maximise the degree of self-sufficiency of food for emergency reasons. We will make agriculture less dependent on state transfers, reduce cost levels and provide industry with new and better income opportunities. Support for the budget shall be made more dependent on production and less land. We will strengthen agriculture through the simplification of laws, regulations and support schemes and remove restrictions that prevent the efficient use of capacity. We want the farmer's right to rule over his own property to be strengthened. The local resource base and the farmers’ own competence and interest should determine the adaptations to a greater extent than public constraints and complex grant schemes. In the dimensioning of economic instruments, we believe it is right to give priority to use that has a resource base for full-time use and a job for professional practitioners. Greater room for action on adaptations and better income opportunities is important for recruiting young people to the industry. Simplification of the structural profile of grants helps both to facilitate good income opportunities for full-time users and to simplify the instruments and to reduce the possibilities for regulatory adjustments. We want to strengthen agriculture by simplifying laws, regulations and support schemes and reviewing concessions, quota schemes and the differentiation of agricultural subsidy rates. Simplification is also highlighted in the Notice. St. 9. For the sake of predictability, changes must be made over time. Good income opportunities are the main means of ensuring adequate recruitment and achieving the objectives of agricultural policy over time. The development of income has always played a crucial role in the framework of agricultural operations. For several decades, the starting point for income policy has been to ensure income development and social conditions for farmers in line with other groups. It is also the basis for the agricultural settlement of the year. The gross income of agriculture consists of revenues from the market and transfers from the country. The structure of the framework is based on estimates for the development of agricultural production, cost development and labour consumption and estimates of wage growth for other groups. In the case of prognosis for growth in production, the agricultural settlement proposal bases the same estimates as the agricultural negotiating committee made in its claim. The agreement between the Right, the Advance Party, the Christian People's Party and the Left wants to facilitate environmentally friendly, sustainable and forward-looking agriculture, with both large and small uses across the country. The aim is to stimulate Norwegian agricultural policy to increase food production with a view to increasing self-sufficiency, including: in the case of Norwegian consumers and preparedness. The cooperation parties also agree on the direction of this agricultural settlement. Agriculture also contributes to important social goods other than food, such as the preservation of Norwegian cultural landscapes, travel and scattered settlements. The operating conditions are different in different parts of the country. Therefore, the cooperation parties consider that a differentiation in the instruments should be maintained, which facilitates a varied use structure and ensures sustainable production of agricultural land throughout the country. In the case of cooperative parties, ownership and the conditions under which farmers operate are important for the purpose of industrial activity. Cooperation parties agree to strengthen resource-based use for full-time use, while maintaining a diverse use structure across the country. On this basis, the cooperation lots have found the basis for making certain adjustments to the structure profile contained in the proposition. It facilitates a simpler instrument system, which maintains a varied structure of use. Good recruitment to agriculture is essential for maintaining an agricultural industry throughout the country and for increasing food production. It is important for the cooperation parties to pursue an agricultural policy that gives young people confidence in the future, the desire and the ability to invest in agriculture as a profession. Cooperation parties agree that the climate agreement should be strengthened and that greenhouse gas mitigation measures, such as: is produced in the agricultural climate, followed up. The parties to the cooperation agreement indicate that the parties to the cooperation agreement agreed that the agricultural contract institute is maintained and that priority should be given to predictability and reforms that can increase profitability. The Parties stress that the negotiator is firm and that it is essential for future settlement not to undermine the Institute. Future settlements should follow common practice by joining the Ministry of Agriculture and Food's proposal on agricultural settlements and not being the subject of negotiations in the Grand Duchy. The cooperation parties agree that the country's offer for future agricultural settlements should be in line with the indications underlying the timing of the year's agricultural settlement and Innst. 8 S for 2013–2014. The cooperation parties agree that the offer made by the State should ensure a predictable framework for agriculture which can help to improve the overall profitability of the industry in the coming years. It is essential that farmers should be able to have income developments in line with other groups. The Parties agree that the Government’s proposal for the Grand Duchy ensures agriculture an average income growth in line with other groups for 2014-2015 and that it is in line with the current income target. The cooperation lots have found the basis for further improvements in the income possibilities for the agricultural settlement of the year, for example: strengthening the basis for recruitment to the industry and increasing food production. The cooperation parties recognise that Norwegian agriculture faces challenges. These must be taken seriously and met with knowledge-based solutions and new research. The cooperation parties agree that the following party-group investigations must be completed during 2015: – agricultural simplification, – the challenges of agriculture in meeting climate change - increased recruitment, The cooperation parties agreed to facilitate conversion and recruitment in agriculture and agreed to allocate 250 million. kr over the budget, compared to Prop. 106 S for 2013–2014. This is done according to the distribution of measures to be taken in the adjustment.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-17.ana.ud213e496	The cord shift is over.
ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-17.ana.ud213e499	Norwegian agricultural policy has received a lot of attention after we got a new and better government. There may be many reasons for this, but at least one thing is certain: there are many who are talking about changes in agricultural policy, but no one has done anything about it before. That's what this government has done. It has been done and will be undertaken to change today's agricultural policy. If we want an agricultural policy in Norway in the future, it is necessary. When current policies work poorly, and most will have changes, these changes must also be made dry. Changes create commitment, and commitment is good. Of course, there will be some who disagree with changes — largely regardless of what these changes are about. What has been proposed by changes in the agricultural settlement of the year are good and necessary measures and simplifications, which, together with future settlements, will stimulate increased production of food and better and simpler management and control. The proposal for a common quota ceiling for all types of milk undertakings is a major advantage. Having two different production ceilings affects the choice of the type of organisation of manufacturers. By harmonising production ceilings for co-operation and single use, the legislation will no longer affect the decision on the choice of organisational form at this point. It will also contribute to simplification related to the management and control of operational cooperation. It is also proposed to liquidate the separate co-operation legislation with number and distance requirements. This makes it possible for milk producers to adapt as best they can without a specific co-operative legislation limiting the number or the distance between them. The aggregation of livestock premium rates, the introduction of grants for more than 50 animals at the same level as for other bovine animals, and the payment of the same premium to sheep, irrespective of the mode of operation, will facilitate legislation. It simplifies control and eliminates incentives to reclassify or move animals between categories of animals or between undertakings. A sheep is a sheep, at least in a grant context. Winding up the turnover requirement to be prepared for production grants instead of raising the bottom deduction is a major advantage. There are currently no conditions under which the turnover limit is to be linked to agricultural activities. Requirements for taxable turnover as basic conditions for grants are also inappropriate both for the applicant and for management. We therefore remove a bureaucratic and inappropriate turnover requirement of NOK 20 000. In order to limit non-agricultural activities, we raise the bottom deduction from NOK 3000 to NOK 6000. Substantial increase in the amount of livestock subsidies is also a change that will benefit Norwegian agriculture. The ceiling on the livestock premium of SEK 280 000 has also made it economically advantageous to split into several enterprises to obtain the most subsidies possible. By doubling the ceiling, it will be less profitable to adapt the legislation and get the most out of it. The purpose of Norwegian agriculture is to produce good Norwegian food, not to extract subsidies. Today, it's 1 million thanks. kr which is the maximum payment for investment grants. We believe that it is the project and local conditions that have to decide what is being paid, and not a limit that has been set at random by the state. These are some of the proposed amendments that will contribute to a positive development in Norwegian agriculture. There are also signs of a powerful government, with a government council that has impact and that will and dare to do something about Norwegian agricultural policy. This is a victory – both for agriculture and the government. Before judging the proposals north and down, you might want to wait and see the results when they come. I think many will be very positively surprised.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-17.ana.ud213e882	First, I would like to thank the rapporteur for a thorough and thorough review of the substance of the matter and the Committee's findings. Agriculture is important for today’s Norway, and agriculture will also have an important place in future Norway. Therefore, the Left will have an innovative, sustainable and environmentally friendly agriculture here to land. Norwegian agriculture is essential to ensure settlement throughout the country, biodiversity, cultural landscapes and food production.Agriculture policy needs to ensure that food production is safe, environmentally friendly and long-term, and it is an overall objective for the Left to maintain a decentralised agriculture – a diverse agriculture with a varied structure of use in the interests of the environment, in the interests of other businesses such as tourism, in the interests of cultural landscapes and animal welfare. The primary wish of the left was for agricultural organisations and the state to agree on an agricultural agreement. We believe it was unfortunate that the breach came at such an early stage in the negotiations, and it is very regrettable that it was not possible for the parties to come together again. Immediate calls were made. It is a long practice for the government to promote its initial offer as a proposal for the Storting in the event of a breach of the negotiations and to adopt it without amendment. The left maintains that future settlement should follow normal practice and not be subject to negotiations in the Storting. But this year it was necessary to make changes to the Storting. In the government's offer, the government had stressed that the financial instruments should be set up in such a way as to give priority to the use that has the potential to be a high level of full-time use. The Left agrees that it is important to create framework conditions that make it possible for those who wish to exploit production capital more effectively and in this way to strengthen income opportunities to do so. However, this effort cannot and will not be at the expense of the profitability of small and medium-sized applications, especially small and medium-sized applications in less productive areas. The Agreement between the Government Parties, the Christian People's Party and the Left has reached agreement to maintain a differentiation in instruments which facilitate a varied use structure and which take into account the differences in the conditions of farming in different parts of the country. Through the agreement between the government parties, the Christian People's Party and the Left, we have made sure to strengthen the incomes of dairy and cereal farmers, where climate is at the heart of it. In addition, we have increased the income of sheep farmers with herds of up to 100 sheep – i.e. average use – and increased the income of farmers with suckler cows and goats. The left considers that it should be possible to be side bottoms in both Lier and Hardanger, and is therefore satisfied that the agreement facilitates an increase in the income of berry beans, vegetable beans and fruit beans. Norwegian agriculture has long traditions of sustainable management that take into account the environment, cultural landscapes and biodiversity. Developments in knowledge and instruments have helped to make activities in many areas more environmentally friendly today than 20–30 years ago. But we're not yet in the goal. In 2012, agricultural greenhouse gas emissions amounted to approximately 4.4 million CO2 equivalent. 8.5 pst. of the total Norwegian emissions. The emissions of agriculture in recent years have been somewhat reduced, and there is a trend on the left that is pleased. The regional environmental programmes cover a central part of the environmental measures and instruments of the agricultural agreement. The aim is the conservation and care of special cultural landscapes and the reduction of pollution to water and air. In the agreement between the government parties, the Christian People's Party and the Left, we ensured that this program is maintained. At the same time, we made sure to reverse some of the government's cuts in the SMEL funds. The purpose of specific environmental measures in agriculture is to preserve the natural and cultural heritage and reduce agricultural pollution. In addition, the government parties, together with the Christian People's Party and the Left, have committed themselves to following up the greenhouse gas reduction instruments identified in the Climate Change Strategy 2020. The greenhouse gas reduction measures described in agriculture can be grouped as follows: biogas production from manure, more efficient use of fertilisers, carbon capture and reduced mineralisation in agricultural soils, and reduced greenhouse gas emissions from the combustion of fossil sources. These measures will follow up on the Left. In addition, government parties, together with the Christian People's Party and the Left, have agreed to launch an investigation into agricultural challenges in response to climate change. The study will highlight the challenges that agriculture will face in terms of changing to a warmer and more humid climate, as well as the question of how agriculture can cope with climate change. Climate action and investigation on climate challenges are what the Left will ensure that they are followed up. We'll take care of that. For the left, it's a goal of 17 pst. food production and consumption in 2020 shall be organic. For sustainable fertility in soil, enough clean water, animal protection, natural diversity and local food security, organic farming is an important alternative and complement to other agriculture. In the Agreement on agricultural settlement, the Left has therefore ensured that the provision of development measures for organic farming under the Agricultural Development Fund is increased by 10 million. kr in relation to the proposal in the State's offer. Norwegian agriculture faces challenges that we need to take seriously. Clearly, there is a need to make changes in Norwegian agricultural policy so that we can meet the challenges of profitability and not least the challenges of recruitment, which we cannot rule out. Unfortunately, there is not a new generation in line to take over all farms in the country. This is a well-known issue that the Left Party, the Christian People's Party and the government parties want to focus on. We agree that an investigation on recruitment should be launched to look at how we can strengthen recruitment to Norwegian agriculture. The two-part Norwegian economy is a challenge for a number of industries and industries in Norway. The left takes the challenge seriously, including for Norwegian agriculture. Another challenge in agriculture is related to soil protection. During the period 2005–2012, it was re-disposaled at the lower edge of 115 000 decars of cultivated and arable land. Cultivated soils accounted for most of the re-disposal. The left is concerned about the high rate of disposal and, in particular, that large parts of the disposal take place in urban areas where the soil and conditions for cereal production are best. There is a need for increased attention on this issue, and the Left therefore looks forward to receiving the government's soil protection plan, as the Stortinget asked earlier in ours, and as an overall nutrition committee was behind it. For the Left, it is important that the challenges in Norwegian agriculture are met with forward-looking solutions based on knowledge and research. That is why it was important for us in the negotiations to have an impact on the preparation of a major communication on future agriculture and forestry. This will form the basis for a knowledge-based debate on a forward-looking agriculture. In this context, it is important that we modernise agriculture and facilitate those who think new. The farmer is a self-employed person with special administrative responsibility. It is important that the entrepreneurial spirit is given an important place in agriculture. The resource base of rural areas needs to be used more widely for economic development and housing, including for people with no direct connection to primary industries. There needs to be more diversity in agriculture. Local authorities need to be given greater opportunities for local adjustment and for looking at agricultural policies in the context of other industrial policies. Farming needs to be given fewer forms and fewer and simpler guidelines. In addition, there is a need for further investment in innovation, research and development in agriculture so that the industry can become even more forward-looking, forward-looking and innovative. The complexity of the legislation and many of the laws of agriculture may in many areas hinder the development of more flexible agriculture, which will enable those who want to invest in the industry to do so. For this reason, the Left looks forward to an analysis of agricultural simplification, which will take full account of legislation and legislation with a view to modernising it. I look forward to a continuing constructive agricultural debate here today. It deserves Norwegian farmers, and it deserves Norwegian agriculture and the Norwegian food industry. I would like to thank the Christian People's Party, especially, and the government parties for the process that has led to what we now have on the table here today.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-17.ana.ud213e1107	Food production is an industrial activity and is to be treated as an industrial activity. Of course, in Norway a lot of meat can be produced in even larger operational buildings based on even more imports of basic products. However, this is becoming less and less related to Norwegian agriculture and Norwegian self-sufficiency. It is in itself good to produce chicken meat in a very efficient way in an operational building located somewhere in the country, but it is not Norwegian food production – it is not Norwegian agriculture. We have to start separating the two factors. A policy that further reinforces a development from the use of Norwegian land and from the use of imported basic products can not possibly strengthen Norwegian agriculture in the long term and cannot possibly strengthen Norwegian self-sufficiency in the long term. Of course, it is not the case that the current government is solely responsible for pursuing such a policy. We all know about wear and tear, etc., which has been going on for many decades, but this is after all the sunmarks a strengthening of such a policy. The Green Party therefore believes that agriculture needs to be strengthened as a sector of society, and we must aim to build up an agriculture that provides many jobs, good income, good resource management and good animal welfare within the framework of good environmental management and a proper climate policy. This we know that an overwhelming part of the population wants. People aren't primarily looking for even cheaper food. UKL 846 million in the long term. in transfers. In the long term, it's a price, not a subsidy, that's going to keep agriculture alive, but we need a long-term strategy to get to it. ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-17.ana.ud213e1430	Initially, I must be allowed to repeat to the representative of Omland that it is actually such that the Council of State, Listhaug, has a significantly greater support for the population than any of the red-green agricultural ministers we have had in the last eight years. I have the pleasure of presenting a whole other matter with much, much greater consensus. I will present the position of the Industry Committee for adoption in Prop. 108 S for 2013–2014 on the reindeer management agreement. Negotiations on the reindeer management agreement for 2014-2015 were initiated on 6 January 2014. The parties reached an agreement on a new agreement at the final negotiating meeting on 18 February. The Agreement shall apply for the period from 1 July 2014 to 30 June 2015. The framework for the 2014/2015 Repurchase Agreement is 111.5 million. kr. This is an increase of 2 million. kr compared to the current agreement after the adopted budget for 2014. The negotiations on the reindeer management agreement have been carried out in line with the government platform and the cooperation agreement with the Left and Christian People's Party. Furthermore, the negotiations are based on the Storting's treatment of last year's reindeer contracts and budget proposals, as well as the situation facing the reindeer industry today. The main objective of the 2014/2015 Repurchase Agreement is to contribute to the development of the repurchase industry as a rational, market-oriented industry that is sustainable in the long term. It has been important to maintain the broad lines of the current grant system, where the direct grants relate to the value of what is produced. The main gravity of the reindeer operation, about 70 p.m., takes place in Finland. The purity in Norway is now approximately the same. 250,000 animals in spring flocks, i.e. Before we get calving. Meat production is about 2000 tonnes per year. The challenges of sanitation are many and complex. In parts of Finnmark, there are still challenges in the relationship between grazing pressure and grazing basis. Therefore, it is important in the management of reindeer management to be aware that the challenges are different between grazing areas and that within individual areas there can also be considerable variation. In the other grazing areas, the challenges are particularly linked to the loss of pure predatory game and other causes of loss, as well as to the safeguarding of the areas in order to maintain the current traditional operation. In all areas it is a challenge to increase the value creation of reindeer management. It is a pleasure to present the opinion of a unanimous committee expressing satisfaction at the agreement between the parties. The Committee also fully supports the continuation of efforts by the government to facilitate an ecologically sustainable industry and the establishment of rules of use for common grazing areas in this context. It is an important point that the reindeer management law and the reindeer management agreement complement each other and draw in the same direction. The Purification Act requires the industry to operate both ecologically, economically and culturally sustainable. Here, there's an obvious danger that you can operate with intersecting targets. The Committee therefore welcomes the fact that it is turning the offer and strengthening the mechanisms that lead to higher slaughter volumes, thereby increasing the industry towards ecological sustainability. The Committee supports the Government when it warns that it will provide for a reduction in the payments of wild game replacements in cases where the performers do not comply with the rules of the Redeployment Act on upper reindeer numbers. It is important to point out that this is rooted in a broad political consensus in the Storting in recent years. Finally, this is the way that reindeer management is in a unique situation. The potential for demand is much greater than the potential for production. In this, there are great opportunities for increased value creation. The Committee therefore agrees with the government that it is central to reindeer management making use of the potential for value creation. A modern reindeer industry requires the industry to have knowledge of ecological sustainability and to have good market competence. This year's reindeer management agreement reinforces the arrangements for the practitioners who will take the industry in that direction.

This does not mean that all dairy farms will produce 900000 litres per year. The resource base is different and provides a basis for different sizes of dairy farming across the country. We need to have room for both small, medium and large dairy farms in Norway. The country's share of free milk quotas is reduced from 50 pst. until 20 pst. This makes it easier for individual producers to build up by purchasing larger quantities of allowances on the private market, rather than for the country to allocate allowances equally to all producers. We also liquidate the specific legislation for joint ventures. All dairy farms are treated equally, regardless of their organisational form. We get the same production ceiling for co-operatives and single use, and at the same time the number and distance rules for co-operatives are repealed. Milk production is one of the carriers of Norwegian agriculture. We'll make it easier for those who want to keep going. One of the assumptions is that those with a willingness to invest have something to invest in. In this case, the availability of milk quotas is essential. Therefore, those changes are significant. Right doesn't just talk about change; we're actually changing. We want to strengthen agriculture by simplifying laws, regulations and support schemes. These changes must take place gradually. This agricultural settlement is a step in the right direction.

The proposal of the Centre Party will guide the development of Health-Norway from Stortinget in detail. It's an intrusive idea to decide different offers at the different hospitals from this house. What is the real competence of the Storting to determine the details and development of local hospitals? I will mention three examples from the time of the red-green government when there were conversions that neither the Stortinget nor the minister intervened in: Acute surgery at Røros Hospital was collected at St. Olav's Hospital. The food department on the lake was converted into a feeding room. Larvik hospital’s emergency functions were moved to Tønsberg, and they are now performing day surgery. Stensby hospital was laid down. Retail management, as the Center Party wants, will deprive the Health-Norway management and directors of the possibility of conversion and therefore also of development. Conversion is intended to provide patients with a better quality of service. It is intended to provide better patient safety. With the proposal of the Centre Party, many processes in health businesses in Norway will be put on hold. This applies to large investment and new construction processes – which many have been waiting for. Not only will it be expensive – you also put an end to completely necessary new investments, whether there is a new psychiatry building in Sørlanden Hospital, a new hospital in the West Viken in Buskerud or a new children’s and youth centre at Haukeland Hospital. I would like to remind you that under the red-green government many necessary changes have been made. A whole of 13 hospitals have been affected, either by the closure of food supplies or emergency services, or by the hospital being closed down. This government has said that food and emergency services should only be discontinued if there is insufficient quality of service. Future quality assurance and robust emergency services are the reasons why the Council of State has decided that the medical emergency function of Rjukan and Kragerø should be discontinued. The decision has created unrest, local involvement and frustration. However, it is important to stress that the Council of State has said that Telemark Health Companies should renew and update their plans for prehospital services. Prehospital services shall have the required quality and capacity before the emergency response is completed. This is what's important to clarify. The entity has been explicitly asked to ensure the quality of the prehospital services. The decision shall ensure the necessary development of two robust emergency hospitals, Notodden and Skien. Together with good prehospital services, these hospitals will provide citizens of Telemark with a better quality of service. There should continue to be specialist outpatient clinics and day-care services in Rjukan and Kragerø. The current hospitalisation was largely determined according to needs over 100 years ago. That time it took quite a while to move from one place to another. Therefore, all treatment had to be done at the local hospital. That's where you could most of the time a little bit of everything. But medical and technological developments have gone rapidly. We are treating patients for diseases today that were not treated 5-10 years ago. However, such treatment requires highly specialised competence and work on interdisciplinary teams. A lot has changed. Today, we're going from one part of the country to another in a mere hour. Helicopters and ambulance planes have made the country shorter. Prehospital services have evolved from being the pure transport of sick people to being highly specialised treatment of patients during the journey. If we're going to put the patient in the center, we're going to have to make changes. The general principles must be adopted by the Stortinget. I therefore propose the following proposals on behalf of the Progress Party and the Right: ‘The government is asked to submit a national health and hospital plan for the Stortinget, where the Stortinget can address the general principles for the development and structure of hospitals and specialist health services during the planning period.’

ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-17.ana.ud213e2803	I see that many have increased expectations as to what the national health and hospital plan should contain. However, I would like to show that I have answered questions about it several times, both in the Questionnaire and in written questions. In the government’s political platform, a firm commitment is made by the government to promote a national health and hospital plan for the Storting in order to create transparency and predictability in the sector. Although the State has been responsible for the hospitals and the rest of the specialist healthcare service for more than 12 years, the Stortinget has not dealt with its own health and hospital plan. The government believes that we need more political governance on how the specialist health service develops. The treatment of the birth message by the Storting shows how we think. There, Stortingen drew up the main lines for organising and developing maternity care, then developed the Health Directorate’s quality requirements, and the health regions subsequently changed their offerings in line with these leaders. This means that the Storting does not decide where to provide food, or which food departments to become feeding rooms. The important thing is that the Stortinget adopts the policy, while the directors of the hospitals are responsible for how the service should be organised and operated within the areas of responsibility they have – and these roles must not be mixed. In the national health and hospital plan, we will outline national principles and frameworks for different types of hospitals and hospital structures. Thereafter, health authorities are required to comply with the principles of professional criteria and quality requirements for the establishment. Health undertakings shall continue to be responsible for planning and implementing the necessary development and conversion work in order to comply with national and professional principles. The National Health and Hospital Plan will include discussions on how to plan them for future needs, thereby allowing the Stortinget to draw up the general principles of specialist health care. Work on the plan has started, and we will have an open planning process with dialogue with all the health regions of the country, users, managers and employees in the sector – and not least patients will be at the table when future hospital services develop. Then I will comment on the changes at the Hospital Telemark. They have come to the conclusion, through thorough investigation, that a collection of somatic emergency activities and daily treatment in fewer places will be the best way to discharge the overall responsibility of the population of Telemark. I will not add that the Storting will decide on the actual offer at each hospital. It will be the responsibility of the board of directors and management within the framework that the Stortinget draws up through the national health and hospital plan. It is the government's responsibility to ensure that health business decisions are within the framework of the Storting. Some of the decisions will also end up as major development projects which will, of course, be submitted to the Stortinget through the State budgets.

That's what this government cleans up." This is very good and very clear in Stortinget. But I'm worried when you give the impression of something else in the media. That is why I am concerned that adequate supervision and response to illegal reservations in the municipal health service are being provided, and that is why we are concerned that the Health Authority carries out a country-wide supervision that monitors doctors' reservation against both possession of abortion and other issues, and that is why we are concerned that the government is promoting proposals for possible legislative changes that the government considers necessary in order to effectively enforce the legislation that prohibits just that type of practice. It is serious when determining which services it provides to its patients at random, as it may in some cases appear. Unfortunately, even though such practices have not been allowed, several examples have emerged, as I mentioned earlier. Although restrictions have been implemented, such as: by clarifications from the previous government in circulars about this very theme, it has not had the desired effect. Therefore, there is a need to take new action. That is why I am pleased that SV submitted the proposal for document 8, and we are in agreement with SV on several proposals, which I am referring to here. So I think it's best for the government parties to explain what their view of the matter is.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-17.ana.ud213e3535	Psychiatric disorders are one of the major public diseases in Norway today. According to the Institute of Public Health, about half of the population will suffer from a mental illness during life, and one in three will suffer in one year. Annual social costs of mental illness in Norway are estimated to be between 60 and 70 billion. kr. I want to quote Anders Skuterud, who is the head of the Norwegian Psychologists' Association. He says, ‘There are still deep gaps in the knowledge of what kind of offers are given to help people with mental disorders’. At the same time, he sends a challenge to his own members to help close the holes. In particular, there is an offer to those with mild mental disorders, and how much of the effort can be done locally and close to the individual. It is good that more and more actors are engaged in developing more knowledge about mental disorders, and in particular about the offer at local level, because it is very diverse. I am pleased that the Committee has had two proposals in parallel on mental health. There is broad consensus in the Committee on the need to strengthen, in particular, the first-line municipal services in the field of mental health, and that this must be done through long-term and long-term measures. The Committee emphasises the particular importance of action aimed at children and young people and health work at school. In addition, it is important to strengthen services in municipalities with a low or no threshold. The aim of lowthreshold services is that all municipality residents can come into contact with healthcare professionals with the competence to prevent, investigate and treat mental disorders. Strengthening the lowthreshold offer will also relieve specialist health services. Furthermore, an overall committee has clear expectations for the major health and public health messages, as well as national health and hospital plans, to be presented in the Stortinget in 2015, and is clear that mental health work and mental health protection must have a reasonable place in these plans, both in terms of capacity, structure and organisation. In terms of how to build up the capacity of the first line service, there is some disagreement within the committee. The majority point to different challenges in municipalities and, therefore, a differentiated approach is important. Some municipalities have already developed a good first line offer in the area, while others have barely started. My own municipality, the municipality of Gjøvik, with 30,000 inhabitants and almost 4,000 students, has only now applied for state grants to recruit its first municipal psychologist in 2014, while the municipality of Fjell outside Bergen, with 23 000 inhabitants, has been targeted since 2006, and today has five employees psychologists and a wide and comprehensive range of services specifically aimed at children, young people and families. The majority point to the fact that, in practice, mental health work is now escalating and that, pending the forthcoming plans in this area, there is no time for the adoption of an escalation plan at present. There is agreement on the need for a long-term and long-term investment. The majority believe that a five-year project-based escalation is not the best solution now, when we are in the middle of important planning processes. The Committee agrees with the need to strengthen the provision of services for children and young people, in particular increased resources for health centres and school health services. However, it is also important to bring forward the educational-psychological service in the municipalities as a particularly important actor who has a different opportunity than pre-school teachers and teachers to follow-up and lead the whole follow-up work around pupils who need to step up their efforts early. Many actors are active and contribute to action, but often there will be long waiting times for both investigation and action pending coordination between actors. There is a need for more research and knowledge about how to better organise efforts as early as possible, and best possible around individuals. It is also important to point to the social professional competence of socionomists and child protection educators. As mental disorders are among the population, it is also natural that this is more reflected in public health work. I am therefore pleased that the government now includes and equates mental health in public health work in the notified public health report. I would also like to add that in a subsequent case, reference is made to a youth health strategy. I also think it is important that we have a majority for that in the UK, and ask the government to come back with a youth health strategy that further puts the spotlight on the special offer to children and young people. I've been around different municipalities lately, both in Mountain, Kongsvinger and Tromsø, and I've heard a lot of good and a little concerns about different services in the municipalities, about special challenges for young people. In Tromsø I was told that the health station in Tromsø has open summer time. They use psychology students and medical students to keep the health center open. They say that's a big need.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-17.ana.ud213e3639	Psychiatric health has become more and more prominent over the past 20 years as a result of major changes in a modern society. Some people owe their careers to me and the desire for success in all areas as a cause of burnout and subsequent mental disorders. Others suffer from severe mental stress from heavy personal experiences, bullying, breakup or loss of work or close to family and friends. Whatever the reason, the new government is taking mental illness very seriously. This is also why the government has identified mental health as one of its main areas of activity. It's necessary when mental disorders are one of our major public diseases and a disorder that very many people will sometime in life become acquainted with, either by themselves being affected or by someone close to you being. Psychiatric disorders are the most expensive of all disease groups in Norway. This makes the need to strengthen prevention and lowthreshold services in municipalities extremely important. It is therefore gratifying that the government has announced a stepping-up for mental health in the municipalities, which will be made visible in the communications on public health, the primary health service of the future and the notified national health and health plan. It underpins the need to strengthen the mental health provision in municipalities by establishing seamless transitions between service levels to avoid unnecessary waiting time and worsening of distress. Many people in need of mental health care also seek refuge from leisure activities, from association with friends and other socialisation. You may feel ashamed not to be at work, but you have a diagnosis that is not visible to everyone. It is easier to get accepted to be home from work with your broken leg – it is reasonably visible. Therefore, it is important that we are all fellow human beings when a colleague, a friend or a family member is affected by mental illness. A low-threshold service is already established in cooperation between the municipality and Modum Bad, which provides the local population with mental health care about the day — when the need arises. It shows that it is possible, and the model can be copied by other municipalities with access to a psychiatric service offer in its vicinity. With regard to the proposal for a five-year municipal health promotion plan, such a plan would have a very different impact on municipalities, because many of them are at very different levels in their development of the mental health service. With the government's already solid commitment in this area, the Progress Party, together with the majority of the Committee, believes that there is no reason to adopt a separate plan for the next five years at this stage. An area that is too easily forgotten when we talk about mental health is the inclusion of – and care for – relatives. When someone suffers from mental illness, it also affects one's closest. There should be greater provision of training and organised work for employers who are encouraged to look favourably at finding solutions for relatives. There may also be some assessment of appropriate representative centres. Both sick and relatives shall meet with understanding, not condemnation. Psychiatric illness is still experienced by many who are shameful and taboo. It shouldn't be like that.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-17.ana.ud213e3861	The Committee has examined a proposal from five representatives of the Christian People's Party for measures to prevent unwanted pregnancy and to reduce the number of abortions. The representatives have put forward ten concrete proposals for decisions. The Committee shares the concern of the proposers that the total number of abortions in Norway is high and has remained stable for many years. A Joint Committee agrees with the proposers on the need for further efforts to prevent unwanted pregnancy and reduce the number of abortions in Norway. The Committee welcomes several of the measures being taken and also endorses the aim of the Council of State, which is set out in the letter to the Committee, that a comprehensive strategy for good sexual health should be established from 2015. The Committee believes that the concrete proposals put forward in this matter must be reconsidered in this work, and that measures to reduce abortion and unwanted pregnancy must become a central element of such a comprehensive strategy. A minority in the Committee, the representatives of the Labour Party, the Christian People's Party, the Central Party and the SV, believe that such a common strategy must be anchored in the Storting through their own announcement of the aggregates and propose this in the vote. The majority of the Committee, the representatives of parties Right, the Progress Party, the Left and the SV, ask the government to develop a health strategy for young people. The Committee endorses or endorses several of the proposals put forward by the representatives by way of comments in the vote. The Committee shares the views of the proposers on the need for sexual education at school and the need for good information and guidance services. The need for increased health care services and student health services is also highlighted by a joint committee. The Committee stresses that the use of long-term prevention methods, abbreviated as LARC, is likely to be under-utilised in Norway among young women, and agrees with the proposers that there is a need for competent measures on LARC among permanents, midwives and health sisters. The proposal to extend the system of subsidised prevention to include LARC is favourable to the Committee. However, a number of the Committee, the Right Party, the Progress Party, the Centre Party and the Left Party point out that this is also a budgetary priority. When it comes to the proposal to extend the system of free contraception to women between 20 and 24 years of age, a joint committee agrees that this is an age group that requires great attention and concrete, targeted measures to reduce the abortion rate. Trials with free prevention for the group have shown good effect. A plurality in the Committee, the Right Party, the Advance Party, the Labour Party and the Center Party believe that it is nevertheless necessary to consider whether it is the right priority of resources to give free prevention to this group of young, adult women. A Joint Committee is concerned that measures such as this do not need to be assessed in isolation, but that one sees it in the context of sexual health in general and measures for the prevention of sexually transmitted diseases. The Committee believes that good financial arrangements are important for women who give birth. Several members of the Committee would like this to be done by stepping up the one-time support. A majority of the Committee would point out that the draft representative essentially deals with prevention and measures to prevent unwanted pregnancy and abortion, without referring to or requiring measures to prevent sexual diseases. The majority think it is important to have a holistic commitment to sexual health, where one also has a focus on prevention, partner responsibility and the reduction of infectious sexually transmitted diseases, for example. the prevention of Klamydia in condom use. The Committee is pleased that the right to prescribe for nurses and midwives should be extended to cover all hormonal contraceptives, and is in favour of the right to prescribe for nurses and midwives for women of all ages. The center party fought for a big message about sexual health and prevention of abortion, and had hoped that the Left would support this, because then we had actually had multiples in Stortinget. We think it is important to get this as a big message, and the left's option is that they have chosen to ask for a strategy for young health. The point is that then we won't get a debate in Stortinget. After all, a strategy has to be developed on the basis of something the Stortinge thinks about a matter. What does the government think the Stortinget thinks about this matter, if one has not thoroughly addressed the issues raised by the Christian People's Party in its proposal for a representative? That's why I think it's very regrettable that the Left couldn't create multiples for us to achieve what I think Left is in agreement with us, that we're going to get this to thorough debate, because it's a very important thing. I'd like to discuss the proposals of the Center Party on this matter and the ones that we're together with other parties about.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-17.ana.ud213e3892	It is an important matter addressed here in a proposal from five representatives of the Christian People's Party. Firstly, we fully share the objectives of the proposers to reduce the number of unwanted pregnancies in Norway and to reduce abortion rates. The Working Party therefore supports many of the proposals put forward in the dossier, such as good guidance and information and easy access to prevention, in particular for the youth group. For us, the commitment to this matter rests on one more premise – that we are uncompromising in the defence of women's right to self-determined abortion. The abortion law has given Norwegian women the opportunity to decide about their own bodies and lives and has therefore been a major step forward. I think that in particular my generation of women know better than anyone else, we who had a youth both before and after the abortion law was passed. That's why it's so important that women who choose to use the right that the law gives them are met with respect, care and understanding. This is therefore an overall proposal in the draft representative we are discussing here. In the Committee's opinion, we have also highlighted FAFUS, family planning and prevention of unwanted pregnancy and abortion among non-Western immigrant women, a project directed by Oslo University Hospital, specifically targeted at women with a minority background. For many young people, the health centre is a widely used lowthreshold service, where a person can apply for a knowledge of prevention and sexual health. In Tromsø you have, for example, had a long-standing offer of nursing care related to the popular Tvibit youth home. That offer is so well incorporated and so reliable that it is often produced as a model for reaching young people with a targeted health service. Therefore, it is important that the government follow up appropriations for the school health services and health centres, so that we are sure that the money is actually used for what they are meant to do. We fear that other urgent tasks in the municipalities will be given priority at the expense of these services. We know that municipalities can get into a lot of trouble when it comes to fulfilling statutory tasks such as: adequate childcare facilities, all the time that this is proven to be underfunded by the government. The working party therefore believes that during an escalation period it must be considered that money should be earmarked for health and school health services, until we see developments moving in the right direction. I address the proposals that the Workers' Party is promoting together with the proposers and in various minority configurations.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-17.ana.ud213e4180	This is a matter for inclusion in the EEA Agreement of a regulation establishing a system for an integrated Europe. For me as a spokesman for the case, it is a pleasure to see a joint committee stand behind the vote on this matter. Connecting Europe Facility, CEF, aims to set up an overarching EU infrastructure investment programme by 2020. Focusing on smart, resilient and fully interconnected transport, energy and digital networks is intended to help strengthen Europe’s internal market. The basic idea of this programme is that it should provide incentives for investment. The EU Commission has estimated that for each invested euro in the infrastructure network, innovative funding could stimulate a new investment of EUR 6 to 15 with a total value of EUR 50 billion. euro. The EU, like the Norwegian government, has seen clear targets for 100 Mbps broadband across the country by 2020. Demanding efforts and coordination are ahead of us to achieve the goal, and expectations related to the digital society are high. The funds linked to the CEF project will contribute to the development of the necessary infrastructure, which will again make technological solutions available within different sectors. The CEF Regulation can contribute to simplification, efficiency and not least to reducing the digital class distinction. The EEA Committee’s decision states that the EEA/EFTA States shall only participate in the telecommunications part of the interconnected Europe system. Norway’s financial participation will therefore be limited to the telecoms area. Participation in the CEF also allows Norwegian public agencies, with partners, to search for means of establishing the Norwegian part of the current digital sector. In short, I would like to show that it is very important for Norway to be actively involved in the cooperative Europe system. It is gratifying to see that this government is following up EU/EEA matters and that it is well seen on the agenda. This allows us to ensure that Norwegian interests are taken care of. Instead of accepting solutions and standards created by the EU Member States, Norway can then participate in designing them itself.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-17.ana.ud213e4227	First, I would like to thank the other committee members and the department of the Council of State of Ketil Solvik-Olsen for their good cooperation in a difficult matter. I feel that we agree on many of the premises and have also experienced that the department has worked hard to find practical solutions. Then it is clear from the wording that the Workers' Party and the Centre reached a different conclusion from the majority. The NSB is in the process of implementing a major rail reform with two key elements: a major change to the ground and a significant increase in the train equipment. This has led to increased frequency on central train routes with maximum track capacity utilisation. For example, it can be mentioned that there are close to 900 trains running daily through the Oslo tunnel. The passenger growth of the NSB in 2013 was 7.3 pst., which is much higher than before, and shows that the NSB has taken good action. However, the introduction of the base route model has not been unproblematic. New train times and frequencies also lead to major changes in the bus offer. Several train stations and bus stops have been shut down. Very many passengers had to change their travel habits. This was also the case at the Steinberg station in the Lower Eiker municipality in Buskerud. The station was temporarily closed from December 2012 but during the treatment of Prop. 1 S for 2012–2013, Stortinget decided that the station should be upgraded and reopened in December 2014. In connection with Innst. 13 S for 2013–2014, Stortinget adopted the reopening of Steinberg station with an estimated cost framework of 200 million. kr. Today, the cost of reopening and upgrading based on congestion around the station has risen to 300 million. kr. In the context of the 2012 budgetary decision, a reservation was made that the Railway Agency should conduct a traffic simulation of the detailed timetable to ensure that the solutions are robust. Traffic simulations indicate that the reopening of Steinberg stations poses major challenges. By letter of 7 April 2014 to the Committee, the Ministry of Transport of the Council of State points out that the maintenance of the Steinberg station poses a risk that: ‘– The local wish for two trains per hour Drammen-Hokksund may be more expensive to realise than if the Steinberg station is shut down. – It is accepted that an already poor punctuality on the route can be further weakened and robustness reduced. – It will not be possible to introduce a uniform two-time frequency on Sørlandsbanen, an offer estimated to generate 100,000 new passengers annually.’ I am pleased that the majority of the committee, all except the member of the Central Party, points out that the Ministry of Transport's opinion is thorough and gives a good account of the benefits and disadvantages of reopening. In this context, I would also like to point out that the Department so strongly emphasises the importance of maintaining a good collective service for the community. I want to commend the strong commitment shown by the local people. This has led to concrete actions being launched in the autumn. Action will be taken to facilitate the stopping of two approaches – two against Oslo in the morning and two from Oslo in the afternoon – at the station from August 2014. Planned punctuality measures shall be implemented and then testing arrangements shall be initiated with regular stoppages at the Steinberg station. The practical experience of the testing scheme will then be an important element of the final decision. This will be an effective way to test whether the railway network’s traffic simulations are in accordance with practice. The Government also proposes comprehensive investment measures and proposes that safeguards such as fences, walkways, simple platform measures and level crossings should be implemented at the station. It is also proposed that measures be taken in tracks and securing facilities in Hokksund to achieve a more efficient crossing of trains there, as well as the removal of the level crossing in Red Street at Gulskogen station in Drammen. This work will be carried out in 2014-2015, and the current time for a testing scheme with a stop at Steinberg station will therefore be at the re-routing in 2015. The Ministry of Transport has started work on improving the provision of collective services in relation to the Steinberg station – among others. dialogue with local authorities, stop express buses and level crossing on Steinberg. The Ministry also wants to establish dialogue with the County Municipality in order to allow local bus services to continue to carry passengers to Mjøndalen and Hokksund stations 3 to 4 km away in each direction. As can be seen from the Ministry's letter, the Railway Agency has taken the task seriously and has worked on a number of options to identify solutions for the Steinberg station. The railway system is an integrated system in which even small changes may have adverse consequences. This applies particularly to high-traffic sections on high-traffic sections of the single-track track.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-17.ana.ud213e4692	Today's big issue is clearly the proposal by the majority of parties to buy roads or other public infrastructure on hire through increased use of so-called public-private cooperation, also known as OPS. In the setting, we note that the f1s, consisting of the government parties, the Christian People's Party and the Left, want more projects to be undertaken using OPS and that the government is to propose a separate strategy to this effect to the Stortinget. From the Workers' Party we will express a scepticism for traditional OPS development. We believe that OPS projects are not a forward-looking and modern way of organising and financing transport projects. OPS is comparable to purchasing on credit. It is debt financing to be served by future appropriations and by future generations. In the vast majority of cases, it is more expensive to buy a hire than to pay in cash and that private financing of public road installations is more expensive, which ultimately has to be paid by the community. Now the committee chairman and the case rapporteur have pointed out what others have said, and then I have to say, what are the others saying about OPS? Even today, in the early days, the driver’s organisation, NAF, said that smart road development is more important than building more roads in private – the state must, of course, take the bill. In Sweden, the Moderates – sister party to the Right – Minister of Finance Anders Borg runs over OPS. He says that private companies will lend us money to pay a higher interest rate, and for them to get a return, but that leads to infrastructure projects becoming much, much more expensive. Toryenes – the sister party of committee leader Linda C. Hofstad Helleland – Boris Johnson, rapporteur in London, has said that in other countries they call it looting, but in London they call it OPS. The truth is that the use of OPS causes infrastructure projects to become much, much more expensive. But that's maybe exactly what's right-wing, that some private companies should be able to evade the flow of community funds. In particular, it should be noted that with additional financial costs and OPS, the Progress Party and the Council of States should pay attention. The Progress Party is precisely the party that is engaged in saving the drivers for unnecessary costs. A keyword here is the interest compensation scheme for toll loans, which they work with. When the Workers' Party says no to OPS, it's simply because it gives less money – not because we're old-fashioned, and not because we don't want to. Therefore, the working party model for better road construction is rapid and rational public deployment without the additional costs involved in OPS. We have to put the money on the table. But of course we are open to new and smart models for road construction. I would also like to highlight the decision to introduce mandatory heavy duty vehicle bombing, which is not least important in order to provide for equivalent conditions of competition in the transport sector. We in the Workers' Party believe that the introduction of mandatory pieces in all vehicles and as a form of payment on the national roads must be of high priority. This is about an implementation method, and we can reduce the cost of finance in this way, and we can find solutions where the state pays off the main debt very quickly, but holds back a bit of the loan, so that the obligations of the operator or the road builder for future maintenance are implemented, and that you do not pay everything right away. It's a common household principle for everyone who cleans up their house – not to pay the craftsmen monthly, but to pay when they're done.

It is an important first step for the establishment of double tracks all the way from Oslo to Halden, with a view to completing double tracks to Gothenburg. It's important for a lot of reasons. On the one hand, we need increased capacity for passenger transport on the route. On the other hand, this capacity could also facilitate more transport of goods. It is known that one of our most important ports today is in Gothenburg, Sweden. We also need to facilitate the transport of goods to Gothenburg both for environmental reasons and in order to secure jobs. At the same time, the rail track in Ski divides into a western and an eastern line. There has been a strong local commitment from both rapporteurs and commuters to put in place a connection in Ski. This also resulted in a comment being submitted by the majority of the Committee. The new proposal in the case, it solves the proposal, addresses this in a good way. It will be beneficial for the development of this region around the east line to ensure that a connection is maintained. We as a society need to invest enough in a good rail network towards the big cities, so that we are ready for the future growth of passengers and freight transport. We're not going to solve this with OPS, which becomes a more expensive solution, and which gives us less railway for money. It is good that we see in this case that there will be an extension that will ensure development and growth for the region by starting the construction of double tracks and facilitating connection to the eastern line.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-17.ana.ud213e5248	I have to admit that this is a matter for which it has been a bit strange to be the case rapporteur, but I would like to start by thanking the Committee for a proper and good treatment. The discussion about data storage rules and the principal aspects of it is both interesting and important. The challenge is that this proposal for a representative is about stopping the implementation of a directive that the Court of Justice of the European Union has known to be invalid and, as such, no longer exists. It would therefore be beneficial for the assessment of the decision of the Court of Justice of the European Union and the implications for Norwegian data storage rules to be debated when the government submits its assessment to the Stortinget. I believe that this is what the Committee has agreed to by means of this proposal for a representative. On 8 April this year, the European Court of Justice delivered its judgment: EU Directive 2006/24 EC, known as the Data Storage Directive, was declared invalid. This means that the directive was repealed and that it is not binding on the Member States, nor can it be implemented in Norwegian law. On 11 April, Prime Minister Erna Solberg sent a letter to the Stortinget stating that the government will not propose a cost-sharing model in the Spring 2014 session, as announced in the State Budget for 2014, and will review the EU judgment to see if there is a need to make changes to the data storage rules from April 2011. The Prime Minister stated that the government would return to the Stortinget case in an appropriate way. In addition, the issue has been raised in Stortinget through written questions, both to the Prime Minister and the Minister of Justice, and explained in an opinion on this draft representative from the Minister of Transport. The discussion on the Data Storage Directive and the rules on data retention in Norway is, of course, marked by the fact that there are difficult balances between privacy on the one hand and effective measures to combat crime on the other. Therefore, the Norwegian rules adopted in 2011 did not take advantage of the whole scope of action provided for by the Data Storage Directive. In particular, the retention period is set at six months, which is the minimum limit of the Directive, followed by a deletion obligation in Norway. More stringent storage security requirements have been imposed, with encryption and closed storage, and there are stricter data disclosure requirements, with legal requirements. As I see it, most people in this room recognize that we need rules for data storage, and that they should be legalised. When there is no longer an EU directive that sets data storage guidelines, it is not in itself an argument for accepting a legal space. On the contrary, this makes clear the need for national legislation in this area. Regardless of approach and principle, it will be clear that we now have a good basis for assessing the impact of the EU judgment, as the government suggests. We will also have sufficient knowledge to assess the need to make changes to the data storage rules from April 2011. I started out that this is a special matter to be the case rapporteur for. The adoption of a decision to stop the implementation of the Data Storage Directive is completely unnecessary. The directive is known to be invalid and no longer exists as such. Similarly, there is no reason to spend a lot of time debating just that. The debate on the Norwegian legislation on data storage and the consequences of the EU judgment, on the other hand, is important, and I look forward to that debate when the government presents its assessments to the Stortinget.
ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-17.ana.ud213e5267	I promise I won't take any more calculations, and if the voice holds, I'll be able to take the whole article too! The digitalisation of society is accelerating, and increasing digitisation has given us many new challenges and issues in terms of privacy and monitoring, as the rapporteur also said. At the same time, digital tracks can also constitute important evidence in serious criminal matters and can prevent the execution of crime and terrorism. Examples of criminal cases here in Norway where traffic data have been important — or actually crucial — evidence are the NOKAS case, the Orderud killings and the robberies of the painting ‘Scry’. It was also in the context of combating serious crime and terrorism that the EU Data Storage Directive was introduced. Now this directive is no longer in force, as the rapporteur said. The European Court of Justice ruled in a judgment that the Directive is invalid because it infringes fundamental rights relating to privacy and the protection of personal data. It is then important to add that in the Norwegian decision we made provision for a much stricter regulation of the privacy of the directive than the legislation that has been assessed in the judgment of the Court of Justice of the European Union. We chose a minimum storage time of six months and introduced deletion after six months. In addition, strict requirements were adopted for telecommunications operators with regard to storage security and erasure procedures. The Storting Decision also lays down strict rules on access to telecommunications data. Police cannot gain access without a court order, and there must be justified suspicion of crime with a penalty of more than four years in prison, for example. serious drug offences, serious crime, trafficking in human beings, terrorist acts and murders. The proposal for a representative we are discussing now makes a proposal that the government stop Norwegian implementation of the Data Storage Directive. As the rapporteur said, the proposal is really superfluous, all the time we are not going to implement it. However, the government has announced that it will go thoroughly through the judgment of the Court of Justice of the European Union, to see if there is a need to make changes to the Norwegian legislation on data storage from 2011. It is also announced that the government will return to Stortinget in an appropriate manner with the matter, and the Workers' Party looks forward to the government's conclusions in this area. Because there is no doubt that there is a need for legal regulation for data storage. Here we are faced with important fundamental balances between privacy considerations and the protection of general social security. The telecommunications companies are storing traffic data today in order to be able to bill their customers. We need a clear code for the safe storage of data traffic and secure procedures for eradicating this. To what extent does any overview show that this actually works properly at present? We can't have it so it's random how this kind of sensitive information is stored and what degree of security it is around it. Today’s legislation in Norway on storage, storage and disposal in the so-called ecosystem law is far from reassuring, not least for privacy reasons. There is a need for better regulation on how to store these data so that they do not deviate. They need to be regulated by whom to access them and under what conditions – not least when to delete them. The job remains, even if the Data Storage Directive is now gone. The starting point for the Data Storage Directive was that of combating and clarifying serious crime and terrorism. The Labour Party believes that it is important that the PST and the police have all the necessary tools to do this, because the fact that the police have access to telecommunications data under judicial control is important for the protection of public security. No one shall be able to access your and my digital tracks without a suspicion of serious crime and a confession from the court. Privacy needs to be safeguarded through strict access requirements while at the same time safeguarding our common security and preparedness. I think it is responsible that the government is now going through the judgment of the Court of Justice of the European Union and is looking at the need to make changes to the Norwegian data storage legislation. We look forward to the conclusion and thank the rapporteur, the representative of the place, for a very good way to work the matter out in the committee.
