ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-17.ana.ud213e5311	The debate on the Data Storage Directive was long and tough in its time, and it went across the blocks of Norwegian policy. The majority of the population, consisting of the Right Party and the Workers' Party, supported it, while the rest of the Stortinget was opposed. The Christian People's Party said no, because we say yes to a society without unnecessary and unjust surveillance. From our point of view, it was therefore a joyous day for the European Court of Justice to declare the proposed data storage directive invalid, which means that the directive is cancelled. We note that the Prime Minister, in his own letter to the Presidency, states that one will go through the judgment thoroughly to see if there is a need to make changes and come back to the Storting in an appropriate way. The European Court of Justice has the background to its judgment – I think we should note that – characterised the Directive as: ‘a very extensive and particularly serious interference with the fundamental right to respect for privacy and to the protection of personal data, without this intervention being limited to what is necessary’. [[...]] With such a clear characteristic, ‘a postponement to see whether there is a need to make changes to the Norwegian legislation’, as stated in the Prime Minister’s presentation to the Presidentship of the Stortinge, is not sufficient for the Christian People’s Party. We have a clear expectation that the decision of the European Court of Justice will have implications for the content of the national legislation and for the changes made to the Criminal Procedures Act and the Econom Act. Storage of the type required by the Data Storage Directive poses a threat to the privacy of Norwegian citizens and violates important rule of law. So there can always be good and legitimate reasons to take action in this field. Let's admit that. But the small steps, if we're not aware of the developments we're going into, could lead us into a comprehensive surveillance society. The arguments for introducing stricter controls can be good and strong. And yes, consideration must always be weighed against each other. But we must never take our freedom and our fundamental rights for granted. One of them is that society shouldn't be monitoring us for no reason. So now we have the European Court of Justice's assessment of the directive. It's ready. The criticism from the Court of Justice is that the directive covers all types of traffic data and all citizens. As the directive is designed, people will feel that their privacy is actually under constant surveillance, according to the judges. The Directive intervenes in this delicate balance between the individual and the state at the expense of the individual's privacy. I hope the government does not want to get on the wrong side in this type of question. The information society poses new challenges and the police must be allowed to take the necessary measures to detect and prevent crime. However, we must not accept methods that affect the privacy and freedom of innocent people. Democratic States should refrain from the type of totalitarian instruments. The government, and especially the Right, which, in our opinion, was on the wrong side in 2011, now has to take this on board, namely that a new balance point has to be found when considering each other. We need tools to fight crime – we agree – while monitoring needs to be limited. The right went too far in the direction of monitoring in 2011 when the Directive was accepted. Now we see that clearly. A new line must then be drawn, and it is now the responsibility of the government to develop relevant instruments to combat crime, which can at the same time safeguard the dignity and integrity of citizens.

The European Court of Justice came to the conclusion on 8 April this year that the Data Storage Directive was invalid from the date of its entry into force. The judgment also introduces our future rules on data storage. In this way, it has already been concluded in terms of the very proposal that we have to deal with today. The fight against serious crime is important but cannot be carried out in the manner provided by the Data Storage Directive. The Data Storage Directive as proposed and adopted went too far at the expense of privacy and the protection of personal data. There is no need for a decision to stop implementation since the Directive no longer exists. However, the main purpose of the Data Storage Directive was to harmonise the provisions of the Member States on data storage, and I think it is important that Norway has an interest in having good cooperation with our neighbouring countries on the storage of this type of data – not least in order to protect our citizens and their rights. These are challenging and complex issues, for there is no doubt that the objective is good, namely to combat crime, or to solve crime when it has happened. At the same time, it is important that this is well balanced towards precisely privacy considerations. We are closely monitoring how the EU and Europe continue to deal with this. One must also ensure that when one comes with other instruments for the police, privacy considerations must be well taken into account, while taking into account technological developments. Much of the data traffic that led to this was leading when the Data Storage Directive was first developed, is now relatively out of date. Much of today's communication is done on Facebook and the type of media, which the Data Storage Directive did not hit to a great extent. We need to look at how we can provide good tools to the police, while ensuring privacy in a better way than the Data Storage Directive did. I don't think we should be hasty. We work on this in the government and will come back to Stortinget in an appropriate way. But, of course, we are also following the debate that is taking place between the big parties, and we are seeing the points of view that emerge there.
ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-17.ana.ud213e5387	Norway is a honey pot for organized criminals. Norway has no help in dealing with offenders against children on the Internet. If a drug trafficker had today crossed the border with Sweden with 1 kg of heroin in the car, the police would have no clues to follow – because we have not implemented the Data Storage Directive, at least if we were to believe what was said by the adherents of the directive, including the police, during the debate prior to the decision. I take the floor because I am still quite upset about the arguments used in the debate. The opponents of the Directive were called privacy extremists by representatives in this Chamber because they believed that the continuous mass storage of all communications data between all citizens of Norway – in case of criminal activity – is contrary to the basic principles of a liberal legal state. Well, the European Court of Justice gave the extremists the right. I did not take the floor to hound, but to remember that fundamental democratic rights are becoming increasingly less and less lost in war, but voted away with good intentions through majority decisions in national parliaments. Security and freedom are two values that challenge each other. But for me, freedom is not the ultimate point of a liberal state of law, but the very core. Indeed, security is not an independent objective in a state of law, but only a means of ensuring the greatest possible civil liberties. If you turn this around and make security the main goal, then first you undermine the freedoms and then the security itself. Of course, police instruments to investigate terrorism and serious crime should be effective. But these must still be proportional to the threat picture and not undermine the liberal democracy that they are to protect. This directive fell upon the Court of Justice of the European Union. In addition, reports from the EU have established that the Directive has not led to further clarifications of serious, organised crime or terrorism. But here it may be useful to recall that the Norwegian police would use the directive, which was the result of the terrorist acts in New York, London and Madrid, to go even after filers and network mobs. I have been to a very good meeting with the Prime Minister today about being hot against women in public. I welcome all the good suggestions, but even I don't want to use terrorist legislation to go after children who bully other children, or people who illegally share music online. Because of the prestige that was attached to the DLD debate, we have lost several years of constructive debate on what means we actually want to give the police, for example. access to store IP addresses for more than 21 days. This is not linked to the directive, but something that can be opened up – if the government wants to. I support, of course, the committee's position on the decision – to attach the proposal to the protocol. The directive no longer exists, fortunately. We can never regulate ourselves away from any risk of serious crime in an open and free society, but in the attempt to do so quickly, we can regulate ourselves away from democracy as a form of society – it has happened before. ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-17.ana.ud213e5441	We are now going to consider the representative proposal from the workers' party representatives to amend Section 27 of the Road Code, so that tolls can also be used for the necessary external emergency measures. I would like to commend the Committee on Economic and Monetary Affairs and Industrial Policy on an important topic. Tunnel safety has been seen to be an ongoing theme, and there is a need to make assessments of how we ensure preparedness in a good way. There are many different reasons for this, but unfortunately there have been accidents in recent years that have put the case on the case map. During the committee's discussion, we have obtained answers from the Council of State on how the law works today, and what is inside it. The short version of it is that for the development of new projects financed by tolls, tolls can also be used for emergency measures, as referred to in the proposal here. In the case of tunnels already completed and in operation, the current system is such that the financing of repairs, possibly additional fire service emergency equipment, is to be paid by tunnel owners and it is the Fire and Explosion Protection Act that regulates who is responsible for the various technical and organisational measures for fire safety of road tunnels. A distinction is made between those who are to be built and who are toll-financed and those who are already in service and for whom a financial facility has been adopted. That is precisely the distinction which is also a little bit of the distinction in the Committee's work on the proposal. The intention is good. What may be a challenge is that where there is already an agreed financing facility, where it is already in operation, it is necessary to open up a changed gap for the tolls in order to be able to finance an additional emergency response. If there is a new case, a new project, it may be within the scope of the decision taken in the specific case. What some of the batches have pointed out is that there may be a need for further clarification in the Road Law in relation to precisely the tunnels to be built, that you specify that there is actually room for it. In addition, the Labour Party has argued in its arguments, comments and proposals that it should also apply to projects already in operation. The right-hand side and the front-hand side have a note pointing to reinforced allocations for tunnels. In addition to that, I, as the rapporteur, have argued for the comments made by the Central Party, the Labour Party and the Left in terms of the need for clarification, and that it is important that the Council of State looks at the needs for securing the tunnels that are already financed, started and operated. ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-17.ana.ud213e5629	We have many tunnels in Norway, and many of these are long. One of the worst scenarios is fire in a vehicle train in these long tunnels. It's not at least a fireman's nightmare. As a fireman, you are prepared to do what you can to save both life and material. This applies, as I said, to many places in Norway, and it has not been given that you have a well-equipped fire department or have sufficient resources to provide the necessary equipment to build these tunnels — or where you have the tunnels today. A report issued in October 2013 was issued by the State's Committee on Transport following the fire in the Oslofjord tunnel. This study has identified five major safety concerns that have contributed to a deterioration in the systemic safety of the Oslo soil tunnel and that have led road users to be caught in the smoke. I want to quote two of these problems: ‘The safety level of the Oslofd tunnel through standby and safety equipment was not adequately adjusted to traffic growth and composition’.» And on the other hand, ‘The fire and rescue preparedness of the Oslofd tunnel was not dimensioned, equipped or organised in relation to what can be expected as regards the location and size of fires in the tunnel.’» The fire in the Oslo soil tunnel, and some later the fire in the Gudvanga tunnel, testifies to a catastrophic potential in heavy-duty vehicle fires in long one-way tunnels with subsequent smoke. In the light of this study, I am concerned that the fire risk in single-loop tunnels meets with priorities that only ensure a minimum level of safety. This also shows that, after the tunnel has been completed, needs may arise that require new safety measures. Today it is by building openings to use tolls to finance the strengthening of safety, also for the fire service. However, there is no opening to apply tolls to such measures on projects that are completed and in operation and that still have toll collection. It is clear that action should be taken in the future and the necessary means should be made available for the local fire services to be invested near those risk tunnels in the necessary equipment and trained to deal with such situations better than is the case today — both at the construction stage and if necessary at a later stage. I believe that, in the future, the costs have to be taken to a level above the minimum level of safety, so that equipment and training in the construction of such tunnels are satisfactory. In addition to this, it is necessary to have a system of renewal — and to be able to meet needs arising at a later stage. This can be done by voting in favour of the Workers' Party's proposal.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-17.ana.ud213e5895	The matter dealt with by the Committee here, a proposal from the Left, is to have a national system of offering an electronic ticket for seamless public travel throughout the country. This is a matter in which the entire committee is behind proposals for decisions. The Committee sees the importance of making the collective offer well available and easy to use for passengers. We want more people to use collective means of transport. Then it has to be easier to choose good, adapted solutions in everyday life. A common ticketing system is likely to increase the use of collective means of transport and could in turn provide a better supply in the transport sector. The Committee considers it important to look at experiences with common ticketing systems that work well in other countries. But we know that such a system can pose challenges for us, especially because of the different price levels we have in our country. The proposers refer to Germany, where they have done so, and that the Germans use the system in more than one hundred German cities. Such a common ticketing system is likely to lead to enhanced competition between operators because alternative travel opportunities can be made more visible. It is important to facilitate a greater diversity of actors in the transport sector. Such a system may contribute to this. Furthermore, the Committee considers that a common timetable planner, together with a common ticketing system, will make it easier for public transport users. This will be particularly important when crossing the regional borders. We also know that making things easier for most people will be crucial because whether or not people will succeed in streaming to the collective offerings. On my own account, I would add that we have had a similar case for treatment in Stortinget earlier, Document No. 8:55 for 2008-2009 on a national scheme for an electronic ‘free-to-cow’ ticket on public journeys. The case then only got votes from the opposition, as was then the Right Party, the Advance Party, the Christian People's Party and the Left. Finally, this time – as I have said before – there is an overall committee behind the proposal. The proposers – including our good colleague Abid Raja – should be satisfied with this.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-17.ana.ud213e5923	First of all, I am very pleased that the Left has been given a unanimous boost to the idea that there should be a common electronic ticketing system for public transport throughout the country. This means that one should be able to use the same bus and train cards in all counties. That winner is important to the Left, and it feels a bit good, even though it's very late. It is also delicious that the media was very wrong, because they wrote yesterday that on Tuesday the Stortinge was going to adopt this system. Little did they know how happy we are in Stortinget to talk – so happy to talk that we have now talked for over 15 hours, over midnight. Now we're not going to adopt this on Tuesday, I'm counting on, because now it's really Wednesday. However, the winner is important, and that is because the transport sector accounts for one third of the greenhouse gas emissions in Norway. In many places there is a lot of queue on the roads. The left and I have promoted this because we are concerned that it will be easier to make green choices in everyday life. The systems must not prevent people from travelling collectively. In particular, the current lack of a common system for travellers in the East coming from another state and entering Oslo and Akershus by train or express bus is problematic. In other parts of the country, there are also no common timetables, so that effective travel planning for us consumers is difficult, not to say impossible. The collective system must make it easier to plan, pay for and make trips from A to Å. The joy I have of getting through is a little conditional, for this room has made several such decisions earlier on the national travel portal and common ticketing system, but it has stopped. That's a shame, because it's an important and central part of the development of the collective system. Although decisions have been made before and nothing has happened, this time I am more optimistic. Why? Well, because all parties agree that it should be possible to travel seamlessly across the country, and we have a Minister of Transport who has so far shown a willingness to act and act. I hope and believe he will demonstrate this in this case as well. This Decision is intended as a reminder, perhaps a push or a pressure from the Storting, in order to implement it as quickly as possible. I'm going to promise to follow up on what the Council of States is doing to follow up, so that we get a ticket system for public transport. In addition, today – or yesterday – I have asked all the county representatives from the Left to press for regional collectives to adapt to a national system to be tested. One of the reasons it has taken so long is that there are many parties to coordinate. I therefore hope that the other parties will also talk to their state politicians about their involvement in taking responsibility for the implementation of this. In this respect, county municipalities should participate actively in the coordination work, adapt their ticketing systems to the Road Administration’s manual on electronic ticketing and, where appropriate, grant funds for conversion to national systems. In Germany, they are able to have one card that gives access to both trains, buses, city bikes, passenger boats, car sharing schemes, etc. Think about it - one card or one app that gives you national access to all collective schemes! Take the plane from the Kirkenes, land in Oslo, walk straight off the Fly train, take the tram, hire a city bike, or do whatever you want, and you only need one card. You can do without fooling around or looking for ticket systems and trying to figure out what other states have of systems. When they do it in a country like Germany, it should be possible to do it in Norway too — and soon. The Council of State gets this task from a total accumulation today, and I am very pleased that both the rapporteur, the representative of the Labour Party and all other parties have concluded this. Two parties are probably not noticed in the committee, but I guess they will also vote for it, and when a total accumulation gives the Council of State this task, I look forward to rapid and effective progress from the Council of State.

The fact that you can order tickets for this and get everything in one place – on a card or on an app on your mobile – instead of having to go to new machines each time, will make it very easy. So there's another challenge with this, and that's the fare system. Some of the success of Router in the eastern part of the country is that thorough work has been done in the tariff area. A common ticketing system was tried 10–15 years ago, when there were so many different tariff groups and discount groups both in Oslo and in Akershus between the different companies that there was a technical gorcial link to this. If you want technical solutions, you have to make price structures simple, so it is actually possible to program – and predictably. Here's a job to do, and then all the actors have to show good will. But I am pleased with the support that Stortinget gives to the proposal from the Left – in the form that it finally received. It's a very clear marshalling earth for us, and we're going to deliver it as quickly as possible.

The President therefore proposes that the procedure for the draft representative, Document 8:86 S for 2013–2014, be in accordance with point (e) of the second paragraph of Section 39, which implies that the proposal is not being examined.
